    \newsavebox\pandoc@box
    \newcommand*\pandocbounded[1]{%
      \sbox\pandoc@box{#1}%
      % scaling factors for width and height
      \Gscale@div\@tempa\textheight{\dimexpr\ht\pandoc@box+\dp\pandoc@box\relax}%
      \Gscale@div\@tempb\linewidth{\wd\pandoc@box}%
      % select the smaller of both
      \ifdim\@tempb\p@<\@tempa\p@
        \let\@tempa\@tempb
      \fi
      % scaling accordingly (\@tempa < 1)
      \ifdim\@tempa\p@<\p@
        \scalebox{\@tempa}{\usebox\pandoc@box}%
      % scaling not needed, use as it is
      \else
        \usebox{\pandoc@box}%
      \fi
    }

    % For linebreaks inside Verbatim environment from package fancyvrb.
    \makeatletter
        \newbox\Wrappedcontinuationbox
        \newbox\Wrappedvisiblespacebox
        \newcommand*\Wrappedvisiblespace {\textcolor{red}{\textvisiblespace}}
        \newcommand*\Wrappedcontinuationsymbol {\textcolor{red}{\llap{\tiny$\m@th\hookrightarrow$}}}
        \newcommand*\Wrappedcontinuationindent {3ex }
        \newcommand*\Wrappedafterbreak {\kern\Wrappedcontinuationindent\copy\Wrappedcontinuationbox}
        % Take advantage of the already applied Pygments mark-up to insert
        % potential linebreaks for TeX processing.
        %        {, <, #, %, $, ' and ": go to next line.
        %        _, }, ^, &, >, - and ~: stay at end of broken line.
        % Use of \textquotesingle for straight quote.
        \newcommand*\Wrappedbreaksatspecials {%
            \def\PYGZus{\discretionary{\char`\_}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\_}}%
            \def\PYGZob{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\{}{\char`\{}}%
            \def\PYGZcb{\discretionary{\char`\}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\}}}%
            \def\PYGZca{\discretionary{\char`\^}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\^}}%
            \def\PYGZam{\discretionary{\char`\&}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\&}}%
            \def\PYGZlt{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\<}{\char`\<}}%
            \def\PYGZgt{\discretionary{\char`\>}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\>}}%
            \def\PYGZsh{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\#}{\char`\#}}%
            \def\PYGZpc{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\%}{\char`\%}}%
            \def\PYGZdl{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\$}{\char`\$}}%
            \def\PYGZhy{\discretionary{\char`\-}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\-}}%
            \def\PYGZsq{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\textquotesingle}{\textquotesingle}}%
            \def\PYGZdq{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\"}{\char`\"}}%
            \def\PYGZti{\discretionary{\char`\~}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\~}}%
        }
        % Some characters . , ; ? ! / are not pygmentized.
        % This macro makes them "active" and they will insert potential linebreaks
        \newcommand*\Wrappedbreaksatpunct {%
            \lccode`\~`\.\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\.}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\.}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\,\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\,}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\,}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\;\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\;}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\;}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\:\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\:}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\:}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\?\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\?}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\?}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\!\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\!}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\!}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\/\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\/}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\/}}}%
            \catcode`\.\active
            \catcode`\,\active
            \catcode`\;\active
            \catcode`\:\active
            \catcode`\?\active
            \catcode`\!\active
            \catcode`\/\active
            \lccode`\~`\~
        }
    \makeatother

    \let\OriginalVerbatim=\Verbatim
    \makeatletter
    \renewcommand{\Verbatim}[1][1]{%
        %\parskip\z@skip
        \sbox\Wrappedcontinuationbox {\Wrappedcontinuationsymbol}%
        \sbox\Wrappedvisiblespacebox {\FV@SetupFont\Wrappedvisiblespace}%
        \def\FancyVerbFormatLine ##1{\hsize\linewidth
            \vtop{\raggedright\hyphenpenalty\z@\exhyphenpenalty\z@
                \doublehyphendemerits\z@\finalhyphendemerits\z@
                \strut ##1\strut}%
        }%
        % If the linebreak is at a space, the latter will be displayed as visible
        % space at end of first line, and a continuation symbol starts next line.
        % Stretch/shrink are however usually zero for typewriter font.
        \def\FV@Space {%
            \nobreak\hskip\z@ plus\fontdimen3\font minus\fontdimen4\font
            \discretionary{\copy\Wrappedvisiblespacebox}{\Wrappedafterbreak}
            {\kern\fontdimen2\font}%
        }%

        % Allow breaks at special characters using \PYG... macros.
        \Wrappedbreaksatspecials
        % Breaks at punctuation characters . , ; ? ! and / need catcode=\active
        \OriginalVerbatim[#1,codes*=\Wrappedbreaksatpunct]%
    }

constant ensures reproducibility throughout. The scikit-learn library
{[}1{]} is used for all preprocessing, modelling, and evaluation
components.

    \hypertarget{introduction}{%
\section{1. Introduction}\label{introduction}}

Mental health in children is a pressing global public health priority.
Depression in early life impairs educational attainment, social
development, and long-term economic productivity, and these effects are
disproportionately concentrated in low- and middle-income countries
(LMICs) where access to screening, diagnosis, and treatment remains
severely constrained {[}2{]}.

This report presents a binary classification analysis of childhood
depressive symptoms using the UNICEF Multiple Indicator Cluster Survey
(MICS) conducted in Malawi in 2019--2020 {[}3,4{]}. The dataset
integrates information from three linked questionnaires (child-level,
maternal, and household) yielding a rich, multi-domain feature set of
\textbf{82 candidate predictors} spanning child demographics, labour
participation, discipline practices, maternal well-being, household
infrastructure, and socioeconomic status. After feature engineering
(four domain-motivated composite features added) and removal of one
high-missingness variable, \textbf{85 features} are retained for
modelling. After removing records with missing or non-valid responses on
the target variable, 13,036 observations are available for analysis.

\textbf{Target variable:} The survey item \emph{``How often does the
child seem very sad or depressed?''} (FCF26) yields five ordered
response categories: \emph{never}, \emph{a few times a year},
\emph{monthly}, \emph{weekly}, and \emph{daily}. To facilitate binary
discrimination between no depression and any form of depression, these
are binarised: \emph{never} is coded as \textbf{no depression} (0) and
all other responses as \textbf{depression} (1). It is acknowledged that
this collapses meaningful severity gradations into a single positive
class. This yields a near-balanced binary outcome, with approximately
54.7\% of children classified as depressed. The heterogeneity of the
depressed class, ranging from mild episodic sadness to daily chronic
depression, is noted as a methodological limitation.

\textbf{Analytical approach:} The analysis proceeds in three stages.
Firstly, exploratory data analysis (EDA) and feature engineering are
used to assess data quality, visualise key predictor--outcome
relationships, and construct a principled, leakage-free preprocessing
pipeline incorporating ordinal and nominal encoding, imputation,
standardisation, and correlation-based feature selection. Secondly, a
\textbf{Logistic Regression} baseline and a tuned \textbf{Random Forest}
classifier are fit, with hyperparameters selected exclusively via
cross-validation on the training set and final performance evaluated on
a held-out test set. Thirdly, feature importances and model coefficients
are interpreted in terms of their real-world significance, and the
findings are translated into policy-relevant recommendations.

The analysis finds that the two models achieve modest but genuine
discriminative ability (test ROC-AUC \textasciitilde{} 0.63--0.64),
consistent with the inherently multidetermined and weakly separable
nature of the outcome. Household socioeconomic status, maternal
well-being, and exposure to harsh discipline emerge as the most
consistent predictors across both models, suggesting clear targets for
preventive intervention.

    \hypertarget{exploratory-data-analysis-and-feature-engineering}{%
\section{2. Exploratory Data Analysis and Feature
Engineering}\label{exploratory-data-analysis-and-feature-engineering}}

    \hypertarget{data-loading-and-target-construction}{%
\subsection{2.1 Data Loading and Target
Construction}\label{data-loading-and-target-construction}}

The raw dataset is loaded and the working sample is constructed by
removing the 126 records (\textless1\% of the total) where the target
variable FCF26 is either missing or carries the value `NO RESPONSE'.
Four cluster-identifier columns (HH1, HH2, LN, FS4) that carry no
predictive signal are also dropped, along with the original FCF26 column
after the binary target has been created. Two Boolean indicator columns
(CL2, CL12) are converted to integers for downstream compatibility with
sklearn.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{2}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Load raw data}
\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}raw} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{read\PYZus{}csv}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{unicef\PYZus{}malawi.csv}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Raw dataset shape: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}raw}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{shape}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Target binarisation}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} FCF26 scale: NEVER / A FEW TIMES A YEAR / MONTHLY / WEEKLY / DAILY}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Binarisation: NEVER = 0 (no depression), all others = 1 (depression)}
\PY{n}{df} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}raw}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}raw}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{FCF26}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{notna}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)} \PY{o}{\PYZam{}} \PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}raw}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{FCF26}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{!=} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{NO RESPONSE}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{copy}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{depression}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{FCF26}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{!=} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{NEVER}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{astype}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{int}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Drop identifiers and original target}
\PY{n}{ID\PYZus{}COLS} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{HH1}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{HH2}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{LN}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{FS4}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{FCF26}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{drop}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{columns}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{ID\PYZus{}COLS}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{inplace}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Convert boolean columns stored as objects}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{CL2}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{CL2}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{astype}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{int}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{CL12}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{CL12}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{astype}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{int}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Working dataset shape: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{shape}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Target distribution (training sample):}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{depression}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{value\PYZus{}counts}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{rename}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{No Depression (0)}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Depression (1)}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Depression prevalence: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{depression}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{mean}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.1\PYZpc{}}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \hypertarget{train-test-split}{%
\subsection{2.2 Train / Test Split}\label{train-test-split}}

The dataset is partitioned into training (85\%) and test (15\%) subsets
using stratified sampling to preserve class proportions across both
partitions. This split is performed before any EDA, feature engineering,
or preprocessing, ensuring that no information from the test set
influences any modelling decision. All EDA, feature engineering
decisions, and preprocessing parameters are derived from the training
set only. The test set is kept entirely unseen until final model
evaluation. Hyperparameter selection for both models is performed
exclusively via five-fold stratified cross-validation on the training
set.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{3}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Stratified 85 / 15 split}
\PY{n}{X} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{drop}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{columns}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{depression}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{y} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{depression}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}

\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split}\PY{p}{(}
    \PY{n}{X}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}size}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.15}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{RANDOM\PYZus{}STATE}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{stratify}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{y}
\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Training set: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{shape}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{]}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{,}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ rows | }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{mean}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.1\PYZpc{}}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ depressed}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Test set:     }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{shape}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{]}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{,}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ rows | }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{mean}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.1\PYZpc{}}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ depressed}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Keep a copy of raw training data aligned with y for EDA plots}
\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}eda} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{copy}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}eda\PYZus{}raw} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}raw}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{loc}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{index}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{copy}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}  \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} for original FCF26 counts}
\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}eda}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{depression}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{values}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \hypertarget{target-variable-distribution}{%
\subsection{2.3 Target Variable
Distribution}\label{target-variable-distribution}}

    \hypertarget{non-substantive-value-recoding}{%
\subsection{2.4 Non-Substantive Value
Recoding}\label{non-substantive-value-recoding}}

Survey questionnaires routinely produce response codes that denote
refusal, ignorance, or inapplicability, with values such as ``DK''
(don't know), ``NOT ASKED'', or ``REFUSED''. These carry no substantive
information, and are replaced with \texttt{NaN} before any further
processing.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{5}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Replace non\PYZhy{}substantive strings with NaN}
\PY{n}{NON\PYZus{}SUBSTANTIVE} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}
    \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{NO RESPONSE}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{DK}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{DON}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{T KNOW}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{DONT KNOW}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{DK / NO OPINION}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{MISSING}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{NOT ASKED}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{REFUSED}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{NO ANSWER}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{UNKNOWN}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}
\PY{p}{]}

\PY{n}{total\PYZus{}replaced} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+m+mi}{0}
\PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{split} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n}{before} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{split}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{isin}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{NON\PYZus{}SUBSTANTIVE}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sum}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sum}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{split}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{replace}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{NON\PYZus{}SUBSTANTIVE}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{np}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{nan}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{inplace}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{total\PYZus{}replaced} \PY{o}{+}\PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{before}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Total non\PYZhy{}substantive values recoded to NaN: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{total\PYZus{}replaced}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{,}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
Total non-substantive values recoded to NaN: 2,050
    \end{Verbatim}

    \hypertarget{numeric-type-coercion}{%
\subsection{2.5 Numeric Type Coercion}\label{numeric-type-coercion}}

Four variables (CL13, MA2, LS2, WS4) were imported as string objects,
despite containing numeric information. These columns are coerced to
float, and any residual non-numeric strings are silently converted to
\texttt{NaN} via
\texttt{errors=\textquotesingle{}coerce\textquotesingle{}}.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{6}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Coerce string\PYZhy{}encoded numeric columns}
\PY{n}{NUMERIC\PYZus{}COERCE\PYZus{}COLS} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{CL13}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{MA2}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{LS2}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{WS4}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}

\PY{n}{num\PYZus{}before} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{select\PYZus{}dtypes}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{include}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{np}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{number}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{shape}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{col} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{NUMERIC\PYZus{}COERCE\PYZus{}COLS}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{split} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{:}
        \PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{col} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{split}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{columns}\PY{p}{:}
            \PY{n}{split}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{col}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{to\PYZus{}numeric}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{split}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{col}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{errors}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{coerce}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{num\PYZus{}after} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{select\PYZus{}dtypes}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{include}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{np}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{number}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{shape}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{]}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Numeric columns before coercion: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{num\PYZus{}before}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Numeric columns after coercion:  }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{num\PYZus{}after}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{  (+}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{num\PYZus{}after}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{num\PYZus{}before}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ converted)}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
Numeric columns before coercion: 8
Numeric columns after coercion:  12  (+4 converted)
    \end{Verbatim}

    \hypertarget{derived-feature-engineering}{%
\subsection{2.6 Derived Feature
Engineering}\label{derived-feature-engineering}}

Four new features are constructed from the raw variables before any
columns are dropped, ensuring that information from high-missingness
variables (particularly CL3, child labour hours) is retained in derived
form. Each feature captures a domain-theoretic construct not directly
observable in the raw survey items.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{total\_children} is constructed from \texttt{CSURV\ +\ CDEAD}
  to capture cumulative family burden, including bereavement.
\item
  \texttt{child\_labour\_flag} is constructed from
  \texttt{1\ if\ CL3\ \textgreater{}\ 0,\ else\ 0}, a binary indicator
  of any labour participation, to preserve the key qualitative signal
  from CL3 before it is dropped due to high missingness.
\item
  \texttt{wealth\_age} is constructed from \texttt{wscore\ x\ CB3},
  wealth x age interaction term. Poverty effects on mental health
  compound with developmental stage {[}2{]}.
\item
  \texttt{discipline\_score} is constructed from count of harsh methods
  among FCD2D, FCD2E, and FCD2F, motivated by dose-response evidence for
  physical discipline and internalising symptoms {[}5{]}.
\end{itemize}

These features are applied identically to all three data partitions.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{7}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Construct derived features (applied before column drop)}
\PY{k}{def}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nf}{add\PYZus{}derived\PYZus{}features}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}part}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
\PY{+w}{    }\PY{l+s+sd}{\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}Add four domain\PYZhy{}driven engineered features to a data partition.\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}}
    \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}part} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}part}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{copy}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} 1. Total family burden: surviving + deceased children}
    \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}part}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{total\PYZus{}children}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{(}
        \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}part}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{CSURV}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fillna}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{)} \PY{o}{+} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}part}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{CDEAD}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fillna}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{p}{)}
    \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} 2. Child labour indicator — derived before CL3 is dropped}
    \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}part}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{child\PYZus{}labour\PYZus{}flag}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{(}
        \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}part}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{CL3}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fillna}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{)} \PY{o}{\PYZgt{}} \PY{l+m+mi}{0}
    \PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{astype}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{int}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} 3. Wealth × Age interaction}
    \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}part}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{wealth\PYZus{}age}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}part}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{wscore}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{*} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}part}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{CB3}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}
    \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} 4. Harsh discipline composite (count of YES responses across 3 items)}
    \PY{n}{harsh\PYZus{}cols} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{FCD2D}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{FCD2E}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{FCD2F}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}
    \PY{n}{score} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{Series}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{index}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}part}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{index}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{col} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{harsh\PYZus{}cols}\PY{p}{:}
        \PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{col} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}part}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{columns}\PY{p}{:}
            \PY{n}{score} \PY{o}{+}\PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{(}
                \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}part}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{col}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{astype}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{str}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{str}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{upper}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{str}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{strip}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
                \PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{isin}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{YES}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{1}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{TRUE}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{astype}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{int}\PY{p}{)}
            \PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}part}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{discipline\PYZus{}score}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{score}
    \PY{k}{return} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}part}

\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{add\PYZus{}derived\PYZus{}features}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{add\PYZus{}derived\PYZus{}features}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{)}

    \hypertarget{missing-data-analysis-and-filtering}{%
\subsection{2.7 Missing Data Analysis and
Filtering}\label{missing-data-analysis-and-filtering}}

Missing data arise for several structural reasons in this survey
dataset. Some variables are conditional on prior responses (e.g.~CL3,
child labour hours, is asked only of children currently engaged in
labour, making it structurally missing for the majority), while others
reflect genuine non-response or routing logic. Visual inspection of
missingness rates guides two removal decisions.

\textbf{Feature removal:} Following the recommendation that a missing
data proportion exceeding 40\% in a feature typically indicates a
variable unsuitable for standard imputation strategies {[}6,7{]},
features with more than 40\% missing values in the training set are
dropped. Only CL3 (68.3\% missing) exceeds this threshold, and its key
signal has already been captured by \texttt{child\_labour\_flag}.

\textbf{Row removal:} Rows in which more than 20\% of feature values are
missing are dropped from all three partitions. Missing values in rows
below this threshold are subsequently handled by imputation within the
preprocessing pipeline. Critically, both thresholds are determined
exclusively on the training set, and the resulting column and row
indices are then applied to the validation and test sets without
refitting.

    \hypertarget{ordinal-variable-cleaning-and-category-merging}{%
\subsection{2.8 Ordinal Variable Cleaning and Category
Merging}\label{ordinal-variable-cleaning-and-category-merging}}

Thirteen variables are identified as ordinal, which measure constructs
with a meaningful natural order but unequal or unknown interval spacing.
These require preprocessing that preserves rank structure while reducing
unnecessary sparsity from rare or overlapping categories. A
\texttt{clean\_merge\_ordinal()} function is applied uniformly to all
three data partitions.

The key transformations are: (1) recoding genuinely non-applicable
responses as missing (e.g.~`NEVER WALK ALONE AFTER DARK' in the safety
perception variables VT20 and VT21, which is not a position on the
safety scale), (2) merging educationally rare upper-level categories
(e.g.~`HIGHER' education absorbed into `UPPER SECONDARY' for child's
school level, where fewer than 0.5\% of observations fall in the highest
tier), (3) collapsing `A LOT OF DIFFICULTY' with `SOME DIFFICULTY' for
the maternal functioning variables AF11 and AF12, where the `A LOT'
category contains fewer than 15 observations in the training set, and
(4) standardising grade label strings for consistency with the encoder's
category list.

\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{ordinal\PYZus{}cols} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{c} \PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{c} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{ORDINAL\PYZus{}CANDIDATES} \PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{c} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{columns}\PY{p}{]}

\PY{k}{def}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nf}{clean\PYZus{}merge\PYZus{}ordinal}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}in}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{cols}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
\PY{+w}{    }\PY{l+s+sd}{\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}Recode non\PYZhy{}applicable responses and merge rare/overlapping categories.\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}}
    \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}out} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}in}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{copy}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{grade\PYZus{}map} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{CLASS/YEAR/GRADE }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{i}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{GRADE }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{i}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}} \PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{i} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n+nb}{range}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{9}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}
    \PY{n}{grade\PYZus{}map}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{update}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{CLASS/GRADE 6}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{GRADE 6}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
                       \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{CLASS/GRADE 7}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{GRADE 7}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
                       \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{CLASS/GRADE 8}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{GRADE 8}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Non\PYZhy{}applicable \PYZhy{}\PYZgt{} NaN}
    \PY{k}{if} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{WB14}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{cols}\PY{p}{:}
        \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}out}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{WB14}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}out}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{WB14}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{replace}\PY{p}{(}
            \PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{NO SENTENCE IN REQUIRED LANGUAGE / BRAILLE}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{np}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{nan}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{v} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{VT20}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{VT21}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
        \PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{v} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{cols}\PY{p}{:}
            \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}out}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{v}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}out}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{v}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{replace}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{NEVER WALK ALONE AFTER DARK}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{np}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{nan}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{p}{)}

    \hypertarget{nominal-variable-cleaning-and-category-merging}{%
\subsection{2.9 Nominal Variable Cleaning and Category
Merging}\label{nominal-variable-cleaning-and-category-merging}}

Six variables are treated as nominal, which have no inherent order.
Merging semantically similar categories reduces one-hot encoding
sparsity and prevents individual rare categories from receiving zero or
near-zero weight estimates. All groupings are made on substantive public
health grounds rather than statistical convenience.

For drinking water source (WS1), categories are collapsed to three tiers
that reflect the WHO/UNICEF Joint Monitoring Programme hierarchy: piped
water (highest safety), groundwater (boreholes, protected/unprotected
wells and springs), and other water (surface, delivered, purchased, or
rainwater). Sanitation facility type (WS11) is similarly collapsed by
upgrade level. Floor (HC4) and roof (HC5) materials are grouped into
`basic' and `finished' tiers, following the established convention for
housing quality as a poverty proxy {[}3{]}. Ethnicity categories with
very low frequency are grouped to prevent near-zero class support in the
one-hot encoded matrix.

        \PY{n}{hc5} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{k}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{BASIC ROOF}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}} \PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{k} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{THATCH / PALM LEAF}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{RUSTIC MAT}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{NO ROOF}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}
        \PY{n}{hc5}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{update}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{k}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{FINISHED ROOF}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}} \PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{k} \PY{o+ow}{in}
                    \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{IRON SHEETS / METAL / TIN}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{CEMENT}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{ROOFING SHINGLES}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}
                     \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{WOOD PLANKS}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{CALAMINE / CEMENT FIBRE}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{p}{)}
        \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}out}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{HC5}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}out}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{HC5}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{replace}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{hc5}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{k}{return} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}out}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{cols}\PY{p}{]}

\PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{split} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n}{split}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{nominal\PYZus{}candidates}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{clean\PYZus{}merge\PYZus{}nominal}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{split}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{nominal\PYZus{}candidates}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Nominal columns merged:}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{nominal\PYZus{}candidates}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{col} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{WS1}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{HC4}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{HC5}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s1}{  }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{col}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{col}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{value\PYZus{}counts}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{dropna}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{False}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{to\PYZus{}dict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}

    \begin{center}
    \adjustimage{max size={0.9\linewidth}{0.9\paperheight}}{B300238_B296110_MachineLearninginPython_ICA_files/B300238_B296110_MachineLearninginPython_ICA_28_0.png}
    \end{center}
    { \hspace*{\fill} \\}
    
    \textbf{Figure 2.} Pearson correlation matrix for numeric features and
the binary depression target (training set). All individual correlations
with the target are weak (\textbar r\textbar{} \textless= 0.10),
confirming that no single numeric predictor provides strong linear
separation. Wealth Score shows the strongest association (r = −0.06),
though Pearson r underestimates its true effect given the variable's
right-skewed distribution. The \texttt{Wealth×Age} interaction is
near-perfectly collinear with Wealth Score (r \textasciitilde{} 0.94),
which is benign for Random Forest but inflates LR coefficient variance
for these two features.

    \hypertarget{preprocessing-pipeline}{%
\subsection{2.12 Preprocessing Pipeline}\label{preprocessing-pipeline}}

The preprocessing pipeline is constructed using sklearn's
\texttt{ColumnTransformer} with three parallel sub-pipelines, each
fitted exclusively on the training set and then applied to the test set
to prevent data leakage.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Numeric features} receive median imputation (robust to the
  right-skewed distributions observed in several survey items such as
  wealth score and age) followed by standardisation via
  \texttt{StandardScaler}.
\item
  \textbf{Ordinal features} receive mode imputation followed by
  \texttt{OrdinalEncoder} with explicitly specified category orderings,
  preserving the rank structure defined in the ordinal cleaning step
  above. The
  \texttt{handle\_unknown=\textquotesingle{}use\_encoded\_value\textquotesingle{}}
  option assigns unseen categories a sentinel value of −1 rather than
  raising an error.
\item
  \textbf{Nominal features} receive mode imputation followed by
  \texttt{OneHotEncoder} with
  \texttt{drop=\textquotesingle{}first\textquotesingle{}} to avoid the
  dummy variable trap in Logistic Regression, and
  \texttt{handle\_unknown=\textquotesingle{}ignore\textquotesingle{}} to
  silently zero-out any unseen categories at prediction time.
\end{itemize}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{13}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Define ordered categories for OrdinalEncoder}
\PY{n}{ORDINAL\PYZus{}CATEGORY\PYZus{}DEFS} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{\PYZob{}}
    \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{CB5A}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{ECE}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{PRIMARY}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{LOWER SECONDARY}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{UPPER SECONDARY}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{CB5B}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{GRADE }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{i}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}} \PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{i} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n+nb}{range}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{9}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,}

    \hypertarget{correlation-based-feature-selection}{%
\subsection{2.13 Correlation-Based Feature
Selection}\label{correlation-based-feature-selection}}

Following the preprocessing pipeline, a lightweight Pearson correlation
filter is applied to the processed feature matrix. Features whose
absolute correlation with the binary target falls below 0.005 are
removed. This threshold is calibrated as the product of the maximum
individual feature--target correlation (\textasciitilde0.10) and the
conventional significance level (0.05), following the approach of Ge and
Zhang {[}8{]}. The filter is computed solely on the training set to
prevent leakage, and the resulting feature index is applied to both
partitions.

\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fillna}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.0}\PY{p}{)}

    \hypertarget{model-fitting-and-tuning}{%
\section{3. Model Fitting and Tuning}\label{model-fitting-and-tuning}}

    \hypertarget{evaluation-metrics}{%
\subsection{3.1 Evaluation Metrics}\label{evaluation-metrics}}

Four complementary metrics are reported throughout this section.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{ROC-AUC:} the probability that a randomly chosen depressed
  child is ranked higher than a randomly chosen non-depressed child.
  Threshold-independent and robust to the mild class imbalance.
\item
  \textbf{Average Precision (AP):} the area under the Precision-Recall
  curve. Emphasises the model's ability to identify positive cases
  across all decision thresholds. A random classifier achieves AP equal
  to the positive-class prevalence (\textasciitilde0.547).
\item
  \textbf{Accuracy:} proportion of correct predictions at the default
  0.5 threshold.
\item
  \textbf{F1:} harmonic mean of precision and recall for the depression
  class at the 0.5 threshold.
\end{itemize}

ROC-AUC is the primary optimisation metric for both models.

\prompt{In}{incolor}{15}{\boxspacing}

\PY{+w}{    }\PY{l+s+sd}{\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}Return a dict of four evaluation metrics.\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}}
    \PY{n}{pred} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{proba} \PY{o}{\PYZgt{}}\PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{threshold}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{astype}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{int}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{k}{return} \PY{p}{\PYZob{}}
        \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Model}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{name}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{ROC\PYZhy{}AUC}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n+nb}{round}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{roc\PYZus{}auc\PYZus{}score}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}true}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{proba}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{4}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Avg Precision}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n+nb}{round}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{average\PYZus{}precision\PYZus{}score}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}true}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{proba}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{4}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Accuracy}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n+nb}{round}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{accuracy\PYZus{}score}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}true}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{pred}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{4}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{F1}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n+nb}{round}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{f1\PYZus{}score}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}true}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{pred}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{4}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{evaluate\PYZus{}model() helper defined.}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \hypertarget{baseline-model-logistic-regression}{%
\subsection{3.2 Baseline Model: Logistic
Regression}\label{baseline-model-logistic-regression}}

Logistic Regression with L2 (ridge) regularisation serves as the
interpretable baseline. It provides directly readable coefficients by
quantifying the log-odds change per unit change in each standardised
feature, making it both a performance benchmark and an interpretive
complement to the Random Forest. L2 regularisation is retained
throughout to stabilise estimates in the presence of the
multicollinearity (Figure 2).

The regularisation strength C (where larger C = weaker penalty) is tuned
over a six-point log-uniform grid via five-fold stratified
cross-validation on the training set, with ROC-AUC as the sole scoring
criterion. The value of C that maximises mean CV ROC-AUC is selected.
The \texttt{max\_iter=1000} ensures convergence on the moderately
high-dimensional processed feature matrix. The fitted model is then
evaluated definitively on the held-out test set.

    \textbf{Figure 3.} Per-fold cross-validation ROC-AUC for Logistic
Regression across six regularisation strengths C (log scale). The five
coloured lines represent individual CV folds, the black dashed line
shows the mean CV ROC-AUC across folds, the red dashed vertical line
marks the selected value of C. Fold scores cluster tightly at each C
value, indicating stable generalisation across training partitions.

    \hypertarget{final-model-random-forest}{%
\subsection{3.3 Final Model: Random
Forest}\label{final-model-random-forest}}

Random Forest {[}9{]} is selected as the final model for the following
reasons. Firstly, it constructs an ensemble of decision trees that
captures non-linear relationships and feature interactions that Logistic
Regression cannot represent, which given the complex socioeconomic and
familial determinants of childhood depression, such non-linear effects
are expected. Secondly, it is naturally robust to the multicollinearity
(Figure 2): individual trees are trained on bootstrap samples and random
feature subsets, so correlated predictors do not destabilise the
ensemble.

\textbf{Hyperparameter strategy:} Three hyperparameters are tuned via
five-fold stratified cross-validation on the training set, with ROC-AUC
as the scoring criterion. Each hyperparameter is swept independently
while the remaining two are held at sensible anchor values, and the
value yielding the highest mean CV ROC-AUC is selected:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{n\_estimators}: 50, 100, 200, 300 (anchor value), 400, 500
\item
  \texttt{max\_depth}: 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, unlimited (anchor value)
\item
  \texttt{min\_samples\_leaf}: 1 (anchor value), 2, 5, 10, 20
\end{itemize}

\texttt{max\_features=\textquotesingle{}sqrt\textquotesingle{}}
(standard for classification) is fixed throughout. The test set is not
consulted at any stage of hyperparameter selection. It is acknowledged
that sweeping each hyperparameter independently does not account for
potential interactions between them. A full grid search was considered
but ruled out on computational grounds given the size of the feature
matrix.

\prompt{In}{incolor}{18}{\boxspacing}

\PY{n}{skf\PYZus{}rf} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{StratifiedKFold}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{n\PYZus{}splits}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{5}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{shuffle}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{RANDOM\PYZus{}STATE}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{fold\PYZus{}colors} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsh{}1f77b4}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsh{}ff7f0e}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsh{}2ca02c}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsh{}d62728}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsh{}9467bd}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Hyperparameter ranges to explore}
\PY{n}{n\PYZus{}est\PYZus{}vals} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{50}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{100}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{200}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{300}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{400}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{500}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{depth\PYZus{}vals} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{4}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{6}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{8}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{10}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{12}\PY{p}{,} \PY{k+kc}{None}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{msl\PYZus{}vals} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{2}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{5}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{10}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{20}\PY{p}{]}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Anchor values held fixed when sweeping other parameters}
\PY{n}{ANCHOR\PYZus{}N\PYZus{}EST} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+m+mi}{300}
\PY{n}{ANCHOR\PYZus{}DEPTH} \PY{o}{=} \PY{k+kc}{None}
\PY{n}{ANCHOR\PYZus{}MSL} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}

\PY{k}{def}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nf}{sweep\PYZus{}cv\PYZus{}scores}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{param\PYZus{}vals}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{param\PYZus{}name}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{base\PYZus{}kwargs}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
\PY{+w}{    }\PY{l+s+sd}{\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}For each value in param\PYZus{}vals, run 5\PYZhy{}fold CV and return per\PYZhy{}fold ROC\PYZhy{}AUC scores.\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}}
    \PY{n}{results} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}
    \PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{val} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{param\PYZus{}vals}\PY{p}{:}
        \PY{n}{kwargs} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{o}{*}\PY{n}{base\PYZus{}kwargs}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{param\PYZus{}name}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{val}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}
        \PY{n}{rf\PYZus{}cv}  \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{RandomForestClassifier}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{RANDOM\PYZus{}STATE}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{n\PYZus{}jobs}\PY{o}{=}\PY{o}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,} \PY{o}{*}\PY{o}{*}\PY{n}{kwargs}\PY{p}{)}
        \PY{n}{scores} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{cross\PYZus{}val\PYZus{}score}\PY{p}{(}
            \PY{n}{rf\PYZus{}cv}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train\PYZus{}proc}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,}
            \PY{n}{cv}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{skf\PYZus{}rf}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{scoring}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{roc\PYZus{}auc}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{n\PYZus{}jobs}\PY{o}{=}\PY{o}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}
        \PY{p}{)}
        \PY{n}{results}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{val}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{scores}

    \begin{center}
    \adjustimage{max size={0.9\linewidth}{0.9\paperheight}}{B300238_B296110_MachineLearninginPython_ICA_files/B300238_B296110_MachineLearninginPython_ICA_43_0.png}
    \end{center}
    { \hspace*{\fill} \\}
    
    \textbf{Figure 4.} Per-fold cross-validation ROC-AUC for the Random
Forest as a function of each tuned hyperparameter (five coloured fold
lines, black dashed = mean CV ROC-AUC, red dashed vertical = selected
value). Each panel sweeps one hyperparameter while the remaining two are
fixed at their anchor values. \textbf{(A)} \texttt{n\_estimators}: mean
CV ROC-AUC stabilises beyond approximately 200 trees, confirming
diminishing returns from adding further trees. The selected value
balances stability against computational cost. \textbf{(B)}
\texttt{max\_depth}: performance increases monotonically with depth.
\texttt{max\_depth=12} is selected in preference because it constrains
tree growth, providing a modest regularisation benefit without
sacrificing discriminative performance. \textbf{(C)}
\texttt{min\_samples\_leaf}: mean CV ROC-AUC is relatively stable across
the range tested, confirming that the optimal setting lies in a flat
region of the performance surface and that results are not sensitive to
the precise value chosen within this neighbourhood.

    \hypertarget{final-model-evaluation-on-test-set}{%
\subsection{3.4 Final Model Evaluation on Test
Set}\label{final-model-evaluation-on-test-set}}

The tuned Random Forest is refit on the full training set using the
hyperparameters selected via cross-validation. Both models are then
evaluated on the held-out test set. This is the definitive performance
comparison between the baseline Logistic Regression and the final Random
Forest model.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{20}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Fit final RF on training set with CV\PYZhy{}selected hyperparameters}
\PY{n}{final\PYZus{}rf} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{RandomForestClassifier}\PY{p}{(}
    \PY{n}{n\PYZus{}estimators}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{best\PYZus{}n\PYZus{}est}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{n}{max\PYZus{}depth}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{best\PYZus{}depth}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{n}{min\PYZus{}samples\PYZus{}leaf}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{best\PYZus{}msl}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{n}{max\PYZus{}features}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{sqrt}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{RANDOM\PYZus{}STATE}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{n}{n\PYZus{}jobs}\PY{o}{=}\PY{o}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}
\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{final\PYZus{}rf}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train\PYZus{}proc}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Evaluate both models on TEST set}
\PY{n}{rf\PYZus{}proba\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{final\PYZus{}rf}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict\PYZus{}proba}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}proc}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{:}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{rf\PYZus{}pred\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{final\PYZus{}rf}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}proc}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{rf\PYZus{}results\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{evaluate\PYZus{}model}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{rf\PYZus{}proba\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{name}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Random Forest (test)}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{=== Final Test Set Results ===}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{results\PYZus{}df} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{DataFrame}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{lr\PYZus{}results\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{rf\PYZus{}results\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{set\PYZus{}index}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Model}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{results\PYZus{}df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{to\PYZus{}string}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
=== Final Test Set Results ===
                      ROC-AUC  Avg Precision  Accuracy      F1
Model
LR (test)              0.6325         0.6718    0.5996  0.6555
Random Forest (test)   0.6418         0.6790    0.6027  0.6832
    \end{Verbatim}

    Both models demonstrate genuine discriminative ability above chance
(ROC-AUC \textgreater{} 0.5, Average Precision substantially above the
no-skill baseline, which equals the positive class prevalence). The
Random Forest outperforms Logistic Regression across all four metrics,
with the largest absolute gain in F1 (0.683 vs 0.656), reflecting its
markedly higher sensitivity at the default 0.5 decision threshold. Both
models' moderate absolute performance is attributable to the inherent
data ceiling (Figure 9).

    \hypertarget{interpretation-discussion-and-conclusions}{%
\section{4. Interpretation, Discussion and
Conclusions}\label{interpretation-discussion-and-conclusions}}

    \hypertarget{feature-importances}{%
\subsection{4.1 Feature Importances}\label{feature-importances}}

Mean Decrease in Impurity (MDI) importances from the final Random Forest
quantify the average reduction in node impurity (Gini) achieved by each
feature across all trees. Higher values indicate features that
consistently provide better splits, accounting for both main effects and
interactions. Human-readable labels are assigned to aid policy
communication.

\PY{n}{fig}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{suptitle}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Figure 5: Model Interpretability — RF Importances and LR Coefficients}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}
             \PY{n}{fontsize}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{12}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{tight\PYZus{}layout}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{show}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}

    \begin{center}
    \adjustimage{max size={0.9\linewidth}{0.9\paperheight}}{B300238_B296110_MachineLearninginPython_ICA_files/B300238_B296110_MachineLearninginPython_ICA_50_0.png}
    \end{center}
    { \hspace*{\fill} \\}
    
    \textbf{Figure 5.} Model interpretability outputs for both classifiers.
\textbf{(A)} Top 20 MDI feature importances from the tuned Random
Forest. Wealth Score and the Wealth × Age interaction rank among the top
predictors, confirming material deprivation as a central risk factor.
Mother's Age, Surviving Children, and Mother's Life Satisfaction appear
consistently in the upper tier, consistent with intergenerational
transmission of maternal well-being {[}2{]}. Discipline Harshness Score,
child labour variables, and household infrastructure indicators reflect
correlated poverty pathways {[}5{]}. \textbf{(B)} Top 10 positive
(orange, risk-increasing) and negative (blue, protective) standardised
Logistic Regression coefficients.

Agreement between panels supports a finding as robust, while divergence
signals non-linearity or multicollinearity. Socioeconomic and maternal
well-being variables are prominent in both, confirming their
reliability. Where they diverge: Wealth Score is absent from the LR
top-10 because its coefficient is diluted by collinearity with Wealth ×
Age (Figure 2). Individual harsh discipline items (e.g.~FCD2H, FCD2E)
appear in LR but are absorbed into the composite
\texttt{discipline\_score} in the RF ranking.

    \begin{center}
    \adjustimage{max size={0.9\linewidth}{0.9\paperheight}}{B300238_B296110_MachineLearninginPython_ICA_files/B300238_B296110_MachineLearninginPython_ICA_53_0.png}
    \end{center}
    { \hspace*{\fill} \\}
    
    \textbf{Figures 6--7.} Evaluation curves for both models on the held-out
test set. \textbf{(Figure 6)} ROC curves: both models rise modestly
above the diagonal, confirming genuine discriminative ability. The
Random Forest achieves modestly higher AUC, though the two curves
overlap substantially across the mid-range operating region.
\textbf{(Figure 7)} Precision-Recall curves: both models substantially
exceed the random-classifier baseline, confirming that the models
capture real predictive signal. The elevated precision at very low
recall (top-left region) indicates that each model can confidently
identify a small, high-risk subset, which is a practically important
property for a resource-constrained screening programme where only a
limited number of referrals can be processed.

    \adjustimage{max size={0.9\linewidth}{0.9\paperheight}}{B300238_B296110_MachineLearninginPython_ICA_files/B300238_B296110_MachineLearninginPython_ICA_56_0.png}
    \end{center}
    { \hspace*{\fill} \\}
    
    \begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
LR: Sensitivity=0.697, Specificity=0.482
RF: Sensitivity=0.784, Specificity=0.384
    \end{Verbatim}

    \textbf{Figure 8.} Confusion matrices at the default 0.5 classification
threshold for both models on the test set. \textbf{(A)} Logistic
Regression achieves lower sensitivity (0.697) and higher specificity
(0.482) relative to the Random Forest. \textbf{(B)} Random Forest
achieves higher sensitivity (0.784 vs 0.697) at the cost of lower
specificity (0.384 vs 0.482). In a population screening context, this
trade-off is preferable: missing a depressed child (false negative)
carries greater harm than an unnecessary referral (false positive).

    \hypertarget{class-separability-diagnostic-umap-projection}{%
\subsection{4.4 Class-Separability Diagnostic: UMAP
Projection}\label{class-separability-diagnostic-umap-projection}}

To contextualise the moderate model performance, a UMAP (Uniform
Manifold Approximation and Projection) {[}10{]} projection of the
preprocessed training features is shown below. UMAP preserves local
neighbourhood structure while compressing the high-dimensional feature
matrix into two dimensions. If the two classes form compact,
well-separated clusters in this projection, a well-tuned classifier
should achieve high AUC.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{24}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Figure 9: UMAP class\PYZhy{}separability diagnostic}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} A fresh UMAP is fitted on the processed training data for visualisation only.}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} This does not affect any modelling decisions and is shown for diagnostic purposes.}

\PY{n}{reducer} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{umap}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{UMAP}\PY{p}{(}
    \PY{n}{n\PYZus{}components}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{2}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{n\PYZus{}neighbors}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{30}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{min\PYZus{}dist}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.1}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{n}{metric}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{euclidean}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{RANDOM\PYZus{}STATE}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{n\PYZus{}jobs}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{verbose}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{False}\PY{p}{,}
\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train\PYZus{}clean} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{np}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{nan\PYZus{}to\PYZus{}num}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train\PYZus{}proc}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{nan}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.0}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{posinf}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.0}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{neginf}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.0}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}2d} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{reducer}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit\PYZus{}transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train\PYZus{}clean}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Subsample 4,000 points for visual clarity (full 8,910 over\PYZhy{}plots)}
\PY{n}{rng} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{np}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{random}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{default\PYZus{}rng}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{RANDOM\PYZus{}STATE}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{idx} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{rng}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{choice}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{len}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}2d}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{size}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n+nb}{min}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{4000}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n+nb}{len}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}2d}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{replace}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{False}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{colors\PYZus{}umap} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{np}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{where}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{values}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{idx}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{==} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{ORANGE}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{BLUE}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{fig}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{ax} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{subplots}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{figsize}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{8}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{6}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{ax}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{scatter}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}2d}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{idx}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}2d}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{idx}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,}
           \PY{n}{c}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{colors\PYZus{}umap}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{alpha}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.35}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{s}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{6}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{linewidths}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{)}

           \PY{n}{mpatches}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{Patch}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{facecolor}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{ORANGE}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{label}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Depression}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{ax}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{legend}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{handles}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{handles}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{loc}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{upper right}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{ax}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{set\PYZus{}title}\PY{p}{(}
    \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Figure 9: UMAP 2D Projection of Preprocessed Training Features}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}
    \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{(4,000\PYZhy{}point subsample, diagnostic only)}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}

    \end{center}
    { \hspace*{\fill} \\}
    
    \textbf{Figure 9.} UMAP 2D projection of the preprocessed training
feature matrix (4,000-point random subsample, orange = depressed, blue =
not depressed). The two classes are thoroughly intermixed throughout the
projection: there are no compact, class-homogeneous clusters visible,
and no low-dimensional manifold cleanly separates the two outcomes. This
is direct visual evidence that the moderate ROC-AUC ceiling
(\textasciitilde0.63--0.64) is a property of the data, not a modelling
failure. Depression in children is multiply determined by a complex
interplay of individual, familial, and contextual factors, many of which
are absent from any single survey instrument.

    \hypertarget{discussion}{%
\subsection{4.5 Discussion}\label{discussion}}

The present analysis demonstrates that a principled machine learning
approach applied to population-level survey data can identify meaningful
predictors of childhood depression in a sub-Saharan African context.
Across both models, three broad thematic domains emerge consistently as
drivers of depression risk.

\textbf{Socioeconomic deprivation:} Household wealth score and the
Wealth × Age interaction rank as the top two predictors in the Random
Forest. While these features do not appear in the Logistic Regression
top-10 coefficients, their effects are absorbed into each other due to
near-perfect collinearity (r = 0.94) and both models consistently assign
predictive weight to the wealth domain, confirming material deprivation
as a central and robust risk factor. Children in low-wealth households
face a constellation of chronic stressors (food insecurity,
overcrowding, disrupted schooling, reduced access to healthcare and safe
play environments) that collectively elevate internalising disorder risk
{[}2{]}. The Wealth × Age interaction suggests that these effects
compound with developmental stage: older children have had longer
cumulative exposure to poverty and are better able to perceive their
relative disadvantage, increasing vulnerability. Household
infrastructure variables (water source, flooring, roofing) capture
additional dimensions of material deprivation and are especially
relevant in the Malawian context where rural poverty is spatially
concentrated {[}3{]}. For policymakers, these findings support
wealth-targeted social protection programmes, such as conditional cash
transfers, as potential levers for reducing childhood depression at
population scale.

\textbf{Maternal well-being and family context:} Mother's life
satisfaction, mother's age, and the total number of children (surviving
and deceased) appear consistently in the upper tier of both models'
importance rankings. This pattern is consistent with an
intergenerational transmission mechanism: maternal psychological
distress, poor self-reported well-being, and high family burden impair
caregiving quality, emotional availability, and responsiveness, which in
turn elevates child depression risk {[}2{]}. The presence of deceased
children as a predictor may additionally reflect household exposure to
bereavement and the psychological sequelae of recurrent child mortality,
a context-specific risk pathway of particular relevance in Malawi, which
has historically faced high rates of child mortality. These findings
point toward integrated maternal and child health programmes that
address maternal mental health not only for its direct benefits but as a
child protection strategy.

\textbf{Harsh physical discipline:} The engineered discipline harshness
score and the individual harsh discipline items (shaking, hitting with
an object) feature prominently across both models. This is consistent
with the extensive meta-analytic literature documenting a dose-response
relationship between harsh physical discipline and children's
internalising symptoms, including depression {[}5{]}. Even when
controlling for wealth and other household factors, discipline practices
retain independent predictive value, suggesting that interventions
targeting parenting behaviour (e.g.~positive parenting programmes that
reduce reliance on physical punishment) could yield improvements in
child mental health independently of socioeconomic conditions.

Taken together, these three domains suggest that childhood depression in
Malawi is best understood as a socially embedded condition, shaped by
the intersection of material deprivation, caregiving quality, and
disciplinary practices. This has important implications for how
screening and prevention resources are allocated: rather than targeting
individual child-level characteristics, the strongest signals point
toward household and caregiver characteristics that are accessible
through routine administrative and survey data.

    \hypertarget{limitations}{%
\subsection{4.6 Limitations}\label{limitations}}

Several limitations of the present analysis warrant acknowledgement.

\textbf{Measurement validity:} The target variable is derived from a
caregiver's perception of how often the child seems sad or depressed,
not from a validated clinical instrument such as the Child Depression
Inventory or DSM-based diagnostic interview. This introduces measurement
error in both directions: caregivers may under-report depression in
cultures where emotional expression is stigmatised, or may conflate
normal developmental sadness with clinical depression. Any predictive
model trained on this proxy measure inherits its limitations.

\textbf{Cross-sectional design:} The MICS survey is cross-sectional,
meaning that predictor and outcome variables are measured at the same
point in time. This precludes causal inference: while wealth predicts
depression, it is equally plausible that having a depressed child
increases household economic strain. Longitudinal cohort data would be
necessary to establish temporal precedence.

\textbf{Missing data:} Despite principled handling of missing values
through mode imputation and structural exclusion of high-missingness
columns, the informative missingness of several variables, particularly
FCD5 (physical punishment, asked conditionally) and MA2 (husband's age,
absent for unmarried mothers), is not explicitly modelled.

\textbf{Generalisability:} The model is trained and evaluated on a
single country survey. Malawi's specific socioeconomic profile, ethnic
composition, and cultural context may limit the generalisability of the
estimated feature importances to other LMICs, even those with comparable
overall development indicators.

    \hypertarget{recommendations-and-future-directions}{%
\subsection{4.7 Recommendations and Future
Directions}\label{recommendations-and-future-directions}}

The consistent signals across both models yield clear evidence-based
recommendations. For \textbf{policymakers}, household socioeconomic
indicators, particularly wealth score and access to improved water,
sanitation, and housing, should be incorporated into child mental health
risk-stratification frameworks, with social protection programmes such
as conditional cash transfers considered as potential co-benefit levers.
For \textbf{healthcare workers}, simple maternal well-being assessments
integrated into existing child health contact points (vaccination
visits, growth monitoring, antenatal care) could identify high-risk
mother--child dyads early. For \textbf{parenting programmes},
evidence-based positive discipline interventions that reduce reliance on
physical punishment are indicated by both the raw data patterns and the
model importances.

\textbf{Future research} should: (1) deploy a longitudinal follow-up of
the MICS cohort to establish temporal ordering between key predictors
and depression onset, (2) validate the binarisation threshold against
clinically validated screening instruments in a Malawian subsample, (3)
test external validity on MICS datasets from comparable LMICs such as
Zimbabwe, Zambia, or Mozambique, and (4) explore ordinal regression
models preserving the five-level response structure for finer-grained
risk stratification.

    \hypertarget{generative-ai-statement}{%
\section{5. Generative AI Statement}\label{generative-ai-statement}}

Generative AI tools were used in the following ways: (1) to assist with
refining the narrative sections of the report, and (2) to assist in
identifying code errors and improving code readability.
